2	Network Working Group                                       B. Brinckman
3	Internet-Draft                                                  R. Mohan
4	Intended status: Standards Track                           Cisco Systems
5	Expires: 19 March 2027                                        B. Sanford
6	                                                                 Philips
7	                                                       15 September 2026

9	   An Application Layer Interface for Non-Internet-connected Physical
10	                           Components (NIPC)
11	                        draft-ietf-asdf-nipc-22

13	Abstract

15	   This document describes an API that allows applications to perform
16	   operations against a gateway serving one or more devices described by
17	   an SDF model.  The API consists of a RESTful application layer
18	   interface that performs operations on those devices, as well as a
19	   CBOR-based publish-subscribe interface for streaming data.

195	1.  Introduction

197	1.1.  Motivation

199	   Low-power sensors, actuators, and other connected devices deployed
200	   for building management, healthcare, workplace, manufacturing,
201	   logistics, and hospitality use cases are often resource and battery
202	   constrained.  Many lack native IP connectivity and instead attach via
203	   heterogeneous non-IP operational networks.  Common non-IP protocols
204	   include BLE [BLE53] and Zigbee [Zigbee22].  When IP is available,
205	   constrained application protocols such as CoAP [RFC7252] may be used.
206	   These devices still need to exchange data with IP-based applications.
207	   Accordingly, applications on the IP network obtain telemetry from and
208	   issue operations to such devices through an application-layer
209	   gateway.  This gateway bridges the application network and one or
210	   more separate operational networks where devices are connected,
211	   allowing applications on the IP network to perform operations on
212	   devices connected to these other operational networks.

214	       +-------------+              +---------+              +--------+
215	       | Application |<------------>| Gateway |<------------>| Device |
216	       |             | Application  |         |    Device    |        |
217	       +-------------+   network    +---------+    network   +--------+

219	            Figure 1: Gateway for non-Internet-Connected Devices

221	   There have been efforts to define Gateway functions for devices that
222	   support a particular protocol, such as a BLE GATT REST API for BLE
223	   Gateways ([Gatt-REST-API]), however they have been limited to a
224	   single protocol or a particular use case.  In absence of an open
225	   standard describing how applications on an IP network communicate
226	   with devices on other operational networks, bespoke and vendor-
227	   specific implementations have proliferated.  Each deployment then
228	   requires: (1) defining or adapting yet another API, and (2) deploying
229	   additional gateway functions, increasing operational and integration
230	   cost.  This specification defines a single, extensible application
231	   layer interface for cross-network and cross-protocol device
232	   interaction through a network gateway.  The intent is to support
233	   multiple network and network protocols (and versions) concurrently,
234	   allow incremental addition of new protocols via mapping, and reduce
235	   redundant infrastructure by enabling multiple applications to share
236	   one standardized gateway function.  Furthermore, by leveraging
237	   interaction models, the application and gateway are able to maintain
238	   a protocol-neutral interface, while the gateway handles the protocol-
239	   specific interactions with devices.

241	   A standardized Application Layer Gateway interface has the following
242	   benefits:

244	   1.  Eliminates repeated bespoke integration effort across
245	       deployments.

247	   2.  Avoids deploying multiple overlapping gateway functions for
248	       different networks, protocols or use cases.

250	   3.  Reduces time and operational cost to integrate new networks and
251	       devices.

253	   4.  Allows applications to interact with devices in a protocol-
254	       neutral way, leveraging interaction models.

256	1.2.  NIPC Gateway

258	   A Non-Internet-connected Physical Components (NIPC) gateway is an
259	   application layer gateway (ALG) that implements APIs for applications
260	   to communicate with devices on different networks connected to the
261	   Gateway.  These devices may leverage different protocols, IP based or
262	   non-IP based.  Even when the device network uses IP, the ALG may
263	   still need to act as an intermediary, since the device network may
264	   not be directly reachable from the application network, may use a
265	   different overlay protocol than the one expected by applications, or
266	   may be subject to a different authorization model.  NIPC APIs allow
267	   reading or writing properties of devices, invoking actions on
268	   devices, as well as enabling or disabling events on devices, by means
269	   of a supporting gateway, in a protocol-neutral way.

271	   In order to perform NIPC operations on a device, 2 prerequisites must
272	   be fulfilled:

274	   *  The gateway has access to a device object, that contains its
275	      identity, in the form of a unique UUID and any credentials & trust
276	      material required to communicate with the device.  Provisioning
277	      this device object is out of scope of this document.  It may be
278	      performed via SCIM [RFC7644] with [RFC9944].

280	   *  An interaction model for the class of devices must be available to
281	      the gateway.  This allows the gateway to understand how to
282	      interact with the device in a protocol-neutral way.  The
283	      interaction model is provided to the gateway by means of an SDF
284	      model, as described in [RFC9880].

286	   Once these prerequisites are met, the gateway can resolve an SDF
287	   affordance referenced in the SDF model into the protocol-specific
288	   operations required for that device.

290	   A NIPC gateway provides the following functions:

292	   *  Authentication and authorization of application clients that will
293	      leverage the NIPC APIs.

295	   *  Maintain or have access to a repository of device objects,
296	      including device identity and trust material.

298	   *  Accept and validate SDF interaction model registrations.

300	   *  Expose APIs for property, action, and event operations.

302	   *  Perform implicit connection management to devices where required;
303	      optionally support explicit connection management.

305	   *  Stream events (publish/subscribe) to authorized data applications.

307	   *  Proxy payloads between networks without interpreting or modifying
308	      application data.

310	   *  Operate one or more channels to supported wired or wireless
311	      networks.

313	   *  Optionally provide a bridge between devices on one or more device
314	      networks connected to the NIPC-Gateway.  This may include
315	      translating between different protocols, if multiple protocols are
316	      supported on the device network(s).

318	   The gateway’s role is to provide gateway functions between
319	   application and device networks; it is not intended to be middleware
320	   that inspects, decodes, or transforms device payloads.

322	                            +------------------------------------------+
323	                            |                                          |
324	   +-----------+   Request  |  +---------+                             |
325	   | onboarding+-------------->|  SCIM   |                             |
326	   |    app    |<--------------+ Server  |                             |
327	   +-----------+  Ctrl Endpt|  +---------+                             |
328	                            |                                          |
329	   +-----------+            |  +------------+   +-------+   +--------+ |
330	   |  Control  +----REST------>|    NIPC    +-->|  AP   +-->| Device | |
331	   |     &     |            |  |   Gateway  |   +-------+   +--------+ |
332	   | Telemetry |<--pub/sub-----+            |                          |
333	   |    Apps   |            |  +------------+                          |
334	   +-----------+            |                                          |
335	                            |            Network Deployment            |
336	                            +------------------------------------------+

338	                        Figure 2: Basic Architecture

340	   Figure 2 illustrates the basic components: applications, the NIPC
341	   application-layer gateway (ALG), an access point (AP), and a device
342	   (D).  The applications, ALG, and AP reside on an IP network; the AP
343	   provides a wireless or wired interface to the device.  Applications
344	   often operate in a different administrative domain than the ALG and
345	   AP, so the ALG will have to support authorization.  The ALG bridges
346	   the IP application domain and the device network, be it an IP-based
347	   or non-IP device network.  This enables applications to perform
348	   operations on devices attached to those device networks.
349	   Applications use a JSON-based [RFC8259] RESTful NIPC APIs for
350	   property, action, and event operations, and a CBOR-based [RFC8949]
351	   publish/subscribe interface for event streaming.

353	1.3.  Terminology

355	   The key words "MUST", "MUST NOT", "REQUIRED", "SHALL", "SHALL NOT",
356	   "SHOULD", "SHOULD NOT", "RECOMMENDED", "NOT RECOMMENDED", "MAY", and
357	   "OPTIONAL" in this document are to be interpreted as described in BCP
358	   14 [RFC2119] [RFC8174] when, and only when, they appear in all
359	   capitals, as shown here.

361	1.4.  Glossary

363	   *  NIPC: Non-Internet-connected Physical Components, the application
364	      layer interface defined in this document.

366	   *  NIPC Gateway: An application layer gateway that implements the
367	      NIPC interface.

369	   *  SDF: Semantic Device Format, a standardized format to describe
370	      devices and their interaction models, as defined in [RFC9880].

372	   *  SDF Affordance: An interaction point on a device as defined in an
373	      SDF model.  Examples of affordances are properties, actions, and
374	      events.

376	   *  SDF Global Name: Absolute URI (with fragment) identifying an SDF
377	      affordance.

379	   *  SCIM: System for Cross-domain Identity Management, a standardized
380	      protocol to manage identity information, as defined in [RFC7644].

382	   *  ALG: Application Layer Gateway.

384	   *  IoT: Internet of Things.

386	   *  Protocol Mapping / sdfProtocolMap: Mapping from protocol-neutral
387	      SDF affordances to protocol-specific operations.

389	   *  BLE: Bluetooth Low Energy protocol.

391	   *  Zigbee: Low-power mesh networking protocol.

393	   *  GATT: Generic Attribute Profile used in BLE for
394	      services/characteristics/descriptors.

396	   *  Service (BLE): Top-level GATT grouping of characteristics.

398	   *  Characteristic (BLE): GATT data element supporting read/write/
399	      notify.

401	   *  Descriptor (BLE): Metadata element attached to a characteristic.

403	   *  Bonding (BLE): Procedure to establish trusted, reusable security
404	      keys.

406	   *  Service Discovery (BLE): Procedure to enumerate GATT
407	      services/characteristics/descriptors.

409	   *  Endpoint (Zigbee): Addressable application entity on a Zigbee
410	      device, grouping clusters.

412	   *  Cluster (Zigbee): Group of related attributes and commands defined
413	      by the Zigbee Cluster Library.

415	   *  Attribute (Zigbee): Zigbee Cluster Library data element,
416	      supporting read/write/report access.

418	   *  Device ID / Group ID: UUID identifying a device or a group of
419	      devices.

421	   *  UUID: Universally unique identifier (128-bit).

423	   *  Data Application / Data App: Registered application receiving
424	      streamed event data.

426	   *  MQTT: Publish/subscribe messaging protocol used for streaming.

428	   *  Webhook: HTTP callback endpoint for push delivery.

430	   *  Websocket: Bidirectional TCP-based message channel over HTTP.

432	   *  Publish/Subscribe Interface: Streaming channel for events (CBOR-
433	      encoded payloads).

435	   *  CBOR: Concise Binary Object Representation; compact binary data
436	      format.

438	   *  CDDL: Concise Data Definition Language; schema language for CBOR
439	      data.

441	   *  JSON: JavaScript Object Notation; text encoding used for API
442	      payloads.

444	   *  Access Point (AP): Network element with a radio interface
445	      communicating with devices.

447	2.  Architecture

449	2.1.  Overview

451	   A Non-Internet-Connected Physical Components (NIPC) gateway is an
452	   application-layer gateway (ALG) that exposes APIs enabling
453	   applications to perform operations on devices attached to networks
454	   connected to the gateway.  NIPC defines two API categories:

456	   *  Registrations: register SDF models for classes of devices and
457	      register data applications that receive streaming event data.

459	   *  Operations: perform protocol-neutral device interactions (read/
460	      write properties, invoke actions, enable/disable events) across
461	      heterogeneous networks and protocols.

463	   To execute NIPC operations on a device, both prerequisites MUST be
464	   met:

466	   1.  The NIPC gateway has access to device instance information: The
467	       device object contains its identity, in the form of a unique UUID
468	       and any credentials/trust material required to communicate with
469	       the device (e.g., via SCIM [RFC7644] with [RFC9944]).  This
470	       device object is identified by the device ID referenced in NIPC
471	       API paths.

473	   2.  The NIPC gateway has access to an interaction model (device class
474	       information): An SDF model [RFC9880] is registered, providing
475	       protocol-neutral affordances and mappings to protocol-specific
476	       operations.

478	       +------------+
479	       |    SCIM    |
480	       |   object   |           +----------------+
481	       |    {ID}    +---------->|                |
482	       +------------+           | NIPC Operation |
483	                                |    - Property  |
484	                                |    - Action    |
485	       +------------+           |    - Event     |
486	       |     SDF    +---------->|                |
487	       |    model   |           +----------------+
488	       +------------+

490	                        Figure 3: NIPC prerequisites

492	   Once both prerequisites are met, authorized applications can perform
493	   NIPC operations on devices identified by their IDs.  See Section 10
494	   for authorization details.  NIPC operations act on SDF
495	   affordances—properties, actions, and events defined in the registered
496	   SDF model.  Certain NIPC operations may also be performed on groups
497	   of devices identified by a group ID.

499	2.1.1.  Device instance information

501	   In order for the NIPC gateway to perform operations on a device, it
502	   must have access to the device's instance information.  This includes
503	   the device’s identity and any credentials or trust material required
504	   to communicate with the device.  The device object MUST include a
505	   unique identity (UUID) and sufficient information to bootstrap trust
506	   and establish connectivity, as NIPC operations assume connectivity
507	   can be established without separate API calls.  While provisioning
508	   devices instance information can be performed in various ways, it is
509	   RECOMMENDED to use SCIM [RFC7644] with the device schema [RFC9944],
510	   which defines the necessary attributes and extensions to support
511	   NIPC.  As per Section 4.2 of [RFC7643], group objects may also be
512	   declared, and leveraged in NIPC operations.

514	2.1.2.  Device class information

516	   Device class information is declared through SDF models, as defined
517	   in [RFC9880].  These SDF models define the protocol-neutral
518	   affordances of a class of devices, as well as protocol mappings
519	   [I-D.ietf-asdf-sdf-protocol-mapping] that relate these affordances to
520	   protocol-specific operations.  The SDF model for a class of devices
521	   can be registered through NIPC registration APIs, as described in
522	   Section 2.2.

524	   The SDF model reference and/or data-app registration MAY also be
525	   included in a device's SCIM object.  See [RFC9944] and
526	   [I-D.ietf-asdf-sdf-protocol-mapping] for details.

528	2.2.  NIPC Registrations

530	   NIPC registration APIs allow applications to register objects that
531	   are not tied to specific device instances.

533	   NIPC supports two registration types:

535	   1.  SDF model registration: Registers an SDF interaction model for a
536	       class of devices.

538	   2.  Data application authorization: Authorizes an application to
539	       receive streaming event data.

541	2.2.1.  SDF model registrations

543	   The SDF model for a class of devices determines how a gateway can
544	   interact with these devices in a protocol-neutral way.  To enable
545	   this, the SDF model must contain protocol mappings, mapping protocol-
546	   neutral SDF affordances to protocol-specific operations as defined in
547	   [I-D.ietf-asdf-sdf-protocol-mapping].  The SDF affordances supported
548	   by the device, as well as its protocol-mappings, are provided to the
549	   gateway by means of an SDF model.  SDF models are described in
550	   [RFC9880].

552	2.2.2.  Data application registrations

554	   NIPC operations can enable or disable event reporting on a device.
555	   Events are reported through a publish-subscribe interface.
556	   Applications that are authorized to perform NIPC operations on
557	   devices can define which applications are permitted to receive
558	   streaming event data for that device.  The data-app registrations API
559	   maps an event to an application that is authorized to receive that
560	   data.  The registration also defines what protocol will be used to
561	   deliver the data (e.g., MQTT, webhook, websocket).  This registration
562	   basically allows applications to instruct the gateway to direct event
563	   data-streams to specific data-applications.

565	2.3.  NIPC Operations

567	2.3.1.  Overview

569	   NIPC APIs are exposed over HTTP [RFC9110].  Requests and responses
570	   use JSON [RFC8259] unless another media type is negotiated via
571	   Content-Type and Accept.  A media type for an SDF affordance can also
572	   be stipulated in the SDF ContentFormat data quality, as described in
573	   Section 4.7 of [RFC9880].  The default media type is “application/
574	   nipc+json” (see Section 11.1).  SDF model registrations use
575	   “application/sdf+json”. Property APIs MAY use other media types
576	   appropriate to the property payload.

578	   Failures use Problem Details [RFC9457] with media type application/
579	   problem+json.

581	   NIPC operations are protocol-neutral operations on SDF affordances,
582	   more specifically properties, actions & events.  NIPC operations can
583	   happen against affordances registered in an SDF model.  Operations
584	   reference affordances by their SDF global name.  If the underlying
585	   protocol requires a connection, the gateway establishes and tears
586	   down the connection implicitly unless an explicit connection is
587	   already in place.

589	   NIPC exposes four operation groups:

591	   *  Properties APIs: These APIs allow applications to perform
592	      operations on properties, such as to read or write values to them.

594	   *  Actions APIs: These APIs perform actions on devices, such as
595	      enabling or disabling a feature on a device.

597	   *  Events APIs: These APIs allow apps to enable or disable event
598	      reporting on devices.  Events are reported over the events
599	      publish/subscribe interface.

601	   *  Trigger APIs: These APIs allow an event on one device or group to
602	      trigger an action on another device or group.  Trigger APIs are
603	      not a fundamental operation, but rather tie 2 fundamental
604	      operations together; an event triggers an action.

606	2.3.2.  Properties

608	   Property operations allow clients to read and write values for SDF
609	   properties.

611	   An example of using a property API is reading the property
612	   temperature from a temperature sensor.

614	   Requests and responses use application/nipc+json unless another media
615	   type is negotiated via Content-Type and Accept.  When using JSON,
616	   binary property values are base64-encoded with padding per Section 4
617	   of [RFC4648].  Regardless of media type, the byte order of any multi-
618	   byte values within a binary payload is not specified by NIPC and is
619	   determined by the underlying protocol.  Multiple properties MAY be
620	   read or written in a single request.  When a single property is
621	   addressed via a query parameter, non-JSON media types MAY be used for
622	   the payload.  On success, the response returns either 200 with
623	   per-property status (JSON) or 204 No Content for single, non-JSON
624	   writes.

626	2.3.3.  Actions

628	   Action operations invoke SDF actions on devices.

630	   An example of using an action API is to turn on a lightbulb.

632	   A successful action request returns 202 Accepted with a Location
633	   header referencing the action instance.  Clients poll the instance
634	   URI to obtain status (e.g., IN_PROGRESS, COMPLETED).  Request bodies
635	   are optional and MAY carry action input in a media type appropriate
636	   to the underlying protocol (e.g., octet-stream).

638	2.3.4.  Events

640	   Event operations enable and disable device event reporting.

642	   An example of using an event API receiving an event that a button has
643	   been pressed.

645	   Enabling an event returns 201 Created with a Location header
646	   referencing the event instance.  Disabling an event uses the instance
647	   identifier and returns 204 No Content on success (for a single
648	   device) or 200 Success with a per-device status list (for a group).
649	   Event payloads are delivered via the publish/subscribe interface
650	   encoded in CBOR [RFC8949].

652	2.3.5.  Triggers

654	   Triggers allow an event on one device or group to trigger an action
655	   on another device or group.  Triggers are not protocol-specific.  As
656	   an example, an event on a BLE device can trigger an action on a
657	   Zigbee device.

659	   An example of a Trigger is when a button is pressed (event), a
660	   lightbulb should turn on (action).  Multiple buttons (group) can also
661	   turn on a lightbulb (device).  A single button (device) could also
662	   turn on multiple lights (group).

664	   Creating a trigger returns 201 Created with a Location header
665	   referencing the trigger instance.  Deleting a trigger uses the
666	   instance identifier and returns 204 No Content on success (for a
667	   single device) or 200 Success with a per-device trigger list (for a
668	   group).

670	2.3.6.  Groups

672	   Where supported by the underlying protocol, operations MAY target a
673	   group of devices identified by a group ID.  Responses for group
674	   operations return per-device results; failures for individual devices
675	   are reported using Problem Details entries within the array.

677	2.3.7.  Connection management for NIPC Operations

679	   For protocols that require connection setup, the gateway performs
680	   implicit connection management during an operation (establish on
681	   demand; tear down on completion).  Gateways MAY support explicit
682	   connection management; when an explicit connection is active,
683	   operations reuse it and do not tear it down.  Explicit connection
684	   management is described in Section 4.5.

686	2.3.8.  Extensions

688	   NIPC supports API extensions for compound or specialized operations.
689	   Extensions can execute a set of NIPC operations in a single request
690	   or provide more efficient mechanisms for specific use cases (e.g., a
691	   bulk operation).

693	   Extensions MUST use the “/extensions” path element.  To ensure
694	   interoperability, extensions MUST be registered with IANA as defined
695	   in Section 11.3.1.

697	2.4.  Events publish subscribe interface

699	   Events are delivered via a publish/subscribe interface.  NIPC events
700	   are encoded in CBOR ([RFC8949]) and can be transported over MQTT,
701	   Webhook or Websocket.

703	   CBOR is used for the publish/subscribe interface as Non-IP payloads
704	   are typically binary.  CBOR encodes binary payloads efficiently, and
705	   is more compact than JSON, therefore reducing the amount of data that
706	   needs to be transmitted to the application.

708	   Event types include:

710	   *  Streaming data from devices: Streaming data is activated/
711	      deactivated with the NIPC events API

713	   *  Broadcasts from devices (e.g., advertisements in BLE)

715	   *  Connection events: Devices connecting & disconnecting

717	2.5.  Paths

719	2.5.1.  General

721	   The NIPC HTTP protocol is described in terms of a path relative to a
722	   Base URI.  The Base URI MUST NOT contain a query string, as clients
723	   MAY append additional path information and query parameters as part
724	   of forming the request.  The base URI is a URL that most often
725	   consists of the "https" protocol scheme, a domain name, and an
726	   initial path [RFC3986].  That initial path for NIPC is recommended to
727	   be /nipc.  For example:

729	     "https://example.com/nipc/"

731	   Additionally a version number may be added, for example:

733	     "https://example.com/nipc/v1/"

735	   After the base or version number, the path must contain a collection
736	   identifier.  The collection identifier can be one of the following:

738	   *  /registrations: for NIPC registration APIs

740	   *  /devices: for NIPC operations on devices
741	   *  /groups: for NIPC operations on groups of devices

743	   *  /extensions: for NIPC extension APIs

745	   The well-known URI /.well-known/nipc defined in Section 11.2 can be
746	   used to discover the base path of the NIPC APIs and the supported
747	   versions and extensions.  The response to a GET request on this URI
748	   MUST be a JSON document that contains the base path, and optionally
749	   the supported versions and extension APIs.  The paths MUST be a URI
750	   template as defined in [RFC6570].  The following is an example of a
751	   template defining the NIPC base path as well as supported extensions
752	   on a server.

754	   <CODE BEGINS>
755	   {
756	     "base_path": "/nipc",
757	     "versions": [
758	       "/v1"
759	     ],
760	     "extensions": [
761	       "/extensions/{id}/bulk",
762	       "/extensions/{id}/properties/blob",
763	       "/extensions/{id}/properties/file",
764	       "/extensions/{id}/properties/read/conditional",
765	       "/extensions/{id}/events/conditional"
766	     ]
767	   }
768	   <CODE ENDS>

770	              Figure 4: Example response for /.well-known/nipc

772	   A formal CBOR definition of the well-known response is as follows:

774	   <CODE BEGINS> file "nipc_well_known.cddl"
775	   NipcWellKnown = {
776	     base_path: text,
777	     ? versions: [* uri / text],
778	     ? extensions: [* uri / text]
779	   }
780	   <CODE ENDS>

782	2.5.2.  NIPC Registrations

784	   Registrations leverage the base path + /registrations.  NIPC supports
785	   SDF model registrations and data-app registrations.

787	   paths:

789	   *  /registrations/models

791	   *  /registrations/data-apps

793	2.5.3.  NIPC Operations

795	   Every NIPC Operations API pertains to either a device or group of
796	   devices, identified by an ID, hence the ID must be reflected as the
797	   first parameter in the path.  For example:

799	     "https://example.com/nipc/v1/{id}"

801	   The second parameter in the path refers to the NIPC operation that
802	   the API will perform on the device.  This can be:

804	   *  properties

806	   *  events

808	   *  actions

810	   *  triggers

812	   *  extensions

814	   These are described in Section 2.3.

816	2.6.  Schema

818	   The NIPC schema leans heavily on the SDF schema, as defined in
819	   [RFC9880].  NIPC operations map directly to SDF affordances.

821	2.6.1.  SDF model registrations

823	   To execute NIPC operations, an SDF interaction model for the device
824	   class MUST be registered.  The model MUST include protocol mappings
825	   that relate protocol-neutral SDF affordances to protocol-specific
826	   operations.

828	   Registration is performed via POST /registrations/models with the SDF
829	   model in the request body.  A registered model can be retrieved via
830	   GET /registrations/models using the model identifier (sdfName).

832	2.6.2.  NIPC Operations

834	   NIPC operations require two parameters:

836	   1.  Device ID: the UUID identifying the target device (or group).

838	   2.  sdfName: the SDF global name (absolute URI with fragment) of the
839	       affordance (property, action, or event) on which the operation
840	       acts.

842	2.6.2.1.  Device ID

844	   All NIPC operations are executed against a device or a group of
845	   devices.  Devices or groups of devices are identified by a unique
846	   UUID, adhering to [RFC9562].

848	        +===========+======+======================================+
849	        | Attribute | Type | Example                              |
850	        +===========+======+======================================+
851	        | id        | uuid | 1d3b2c36-8a65-45a6-87c1-bcdbe0a32e30 |
852	        +-----------+------+--------------------------------------+

854	            Table 1: Definition of a device or group of devices

856	   ID is a UUID assigned to uniquely identify a device to the NIPC
857	   Gateway (e.g., by a SCIM server) and the application calling the
858	   APIs.  The NIPC Gateway must have access to a device object
859	   identified by this device ID and the application must store this UUID
860	   in order to use it to identify the device on which it wants to
861	   perform NIPC operations.

863	2.6.2.2.  SDF Name

865	   Operations act on SDF affordances and reference them by SDF global
866	   name—an absolute URI with fragment that includes the namespace.  The
867	   affordance referenced MAY be a property, action, or event; the
868	   reference is carried as a string.

870	   For example:

872	    +================+================================================+
873	    | NIPC Operation | Example SDF Name used in the API               |
874	    +================+================================================+
875	    | Property       | https://example.com/heartrate#/                |
876	    |                | sdfObject/thermostat/sdfProperty/temperature   |
877	    +----------------+------------------------------------------------+
878	    | Event          | https://example.com/heartrate#                 |
879	    |                | /sdfObject/healthsensor/sdfEvent/fallDetected  |
880	    +----------------+------------------------------------------------+
881	    | Action         | https://example.com/heartrate#/                |
882	    |                | sdfObject/thermostat/sdfAction/resetThermostat |
883	    +----------------+------------------------------------------------+

885	               Table 2: Example SDF names for NIPC operations

887	2.6.3.  Parameters

889	   To minimize deployment risk arising from inconsistent URI path
890	   normalization and handling of percent-encoded reserved characters in
891	   path elements (notably “/” encoded as “%2F”) across common HTTP
892	   servers and intermediaries (e.g., Jetty, Tomcat, Apache httpd,
893	   NGINX), NIPC uses query parameters (except the primary {id} path
894	   segment) rather than additional path segments for values that can
895	   legitimately contain reserved characters (such as SDF global names).
896	   Although [RFC3986] and [RFC9110] require that reserved characters not
897	   be normalized or decoded in ways that alter semantics,
898	   misinterpretation of these rules has led to security vulnerabilities
899	   (path confusion, cache poisoning, authorization bypass) and to
900	   conservative default configurations that reject encoded slashes.  In
901	   proxy deployments, relaxing these defaults often triggers security
902	   review friction.  Representing such values in the query component
903	   avoids reliance on tolerant path normalization behavior, reduces
904	   ambiguity for intermediaries, and aligns with prevailing “safe”
905	   operational profiles; a malformed or unsupported parameter can be
906	   rejected with a 4xx status without exposing downstream services to
907	   traversal or normalization discrepancies.

909	2.6.4.  Responses

911	   A NIPC Gateway must respond to a NIPC operation request
912	   synchronously, and provide the result of the completed operation in
913	   the HTTP response.

915	   Exceptions to this are the following:

917	   1.  Extensions: Extension APIs (see Section 5.2) execute compound
918	       operations and thus require the gateway to execute multiple NIPC
919	       operations.  On acceptance, the gateway returns 202 Accepted.
920	       Clients poll the extension URI (GET) for execution status.  If a
921	       callback URI was supplied in the request, the gateway MAY send
922	       the final result upon completion.

924	   2.  Actions: Action requests return 202 Accepted with a Location
925	       header pointing to the action instance used for status tracking,
926	       and a Retry-After header indicating the number of seconds the
927	       client should wait before polling for status.

929	   A failure response must contain an HTTP status code of 4xx or 5xx,
930	   and use [RFC9457] Problem Details with application/problem+json media
931	   type.

933	   All NIPC failure responses must include the following attributes:

935	   *  type: a URI identifying the error (see Section 6)

937	   *  status: the 4xx or 5xx HTTP status code

939	   *  title: a brief, human-readable summary

941	   *  detail: a human-readable explanation specific to this occurrence
942	      Additional attributes MAY be included as permitted by [RFC9457].

944	   <CODE BEGINS> file "failure_response.cddl"
945	   =============== NOTE: '\' line wrapping per RFC 8792 ================

947	   FailureResponse = {
948	     type: FailureTypeURI,
949	     status: uint,
950	     title: text,
951	     detail: text
952	   }

954	   ; Enumerated problem type URIs registered for NIPC
955	   FailureTypeURI = (
956	     "https://www.iana.org/assignments/nipc-problem-types#invalid-id" /
957	     "https://www.iana.org/assignments/nipc-problem-types#invalid-sdf-\
958	                                                                  url" /
959	     "https://www.iana.org/assignments/nipc-problem-types#extension-\
960	                                               operation-not-executed" /
961	     "https://www.iana.org/assignments/nipc-problem-types#sdf-model-\
962	                                                   already-registered" /
963	     "https://www.iana.org/assignments/nipc-problem-types#sdf-model-in-\
964	                                                                  use" /
965	     "https://www.iana.org/assignments/nipc-problem-types#unsupported-\
966	                                                           uri-scheme" /
967	     "https://www.iana.org/assignments/nipc-problem-types#property-not-\
968	                                                             readable" /
969	     "https://www.iana.org/assignments/nipc-problem-types#property-read\
970	                                                              -failed" /
971	     "https://www.iana.org/assignments/nipc-problem-types#property-not-\
972	                                                             writable" /
973	     "https://www.iana.org/assignments/nipc-problem-types#property-\
974	                                                         write-failed" /
975	     "https://www.iana.org/assignments/nipc-problem-types#event-already\
976	                                                             -enabled" /
977	     "https://www.iana.org/assignments/nipc-problem-types#event-not-\
978	                                                              enabled" /
979	     "https://www.iana.org/assignments/nipc-problem-types#event-not-\
980	                                                           registered" /
981	     "https://www.iana.org/assignments/nipc-problem-types#trigger-\
982	                                                      already-enabled" /

984	     "https://www.iana.org/assignments/nipc-problem-types#protocolmap-\
985	                                                ble-already-connected" /
986	     "https://www.iana.org/assignments/nipc-problem-types#protocolmap-\
987	                                                    ble-no-connection" /
988	     "https://www.iana.org/assignments/nipc-problem-types#protocolmap-\
989	                                               ble-connection-timeout" /
990	     "https://www.iana.org/assignments/nipc-problem-types#protocolmap-\
991	                                                   ble-bonding-failed" /
992	     "https://www.iana.org/assignments/nipc-problem-types#protocolmap-\
993	                                                ble-connection-failed" /
994	     "https://www.iana.org/assignments/nipc-problem-types#protocolmap-\
995	                                         ble-service-discovery-failed" /
996	     "https://www.iana.org/assignments/nipc-problem-types#protocolmap-\
997	                                ble-invalid-service-or-characteristic" /
998	     "https://www.iana.org/assignments/nipc-problem-types#protocolmap-\
999	                                            zigbee-connection-timeout" /
1000	     "https://www.iana.org/assignments/nipc-problem-types#protocolmap-\
1001	                                   zigbee-invalid-endpoint-or-cluster" /
1002	     "https://www.iana.org/assignments/nipc-problem-types#extension-\
1003	                                                transmit-invalid-data" /
1004	     "https://www.iana.org/assignments/nipc-problem-types#extension-\
1005	                                                    firmware-rollback" /
1006	     "https://www.iana.org/assignments/nipc-problem-types#extension-\
1007	                                               firmware-update-failed" /
1008	     "about:blank"
1009	   )
1010	   <CODE ENDS>

1012	   Example of a failure response:

1014	   <CODE BEGINS>
1015	   =============== NOTE: '\' line wrapping per RFC 8792 ================

1017	   {
1018	     "type": "https://www.iana.org/assignments/nipc-problem-types#\
1019	                                                            invalid-id",
1020	     "status": 400,
1021	     "title": "Invalid Device ID",
1022	     "detail": "Device ID 1d3b2c36-8a65-45a6-87c1-bcdbe0a32e30 does \
1023	                                           not exist or is not a device"
1024	   }
1025	   <CODE ENDS>

1027	                     Figure 5: Example failure response

1029	3.  NIPC Registration APIs

1031	3.1.  SDF model registrations APIs

1033	   SDF model registration APIs allow applications to register an SDF
1034	   model for a class of devices.  These APIs use the application/
1035	   sdf+json media type, as described in Section 7.1 of [RFC9880].

1037	3.1.1.  Register an SDF model

1039	   Method: POST /registrations/models

1041	   Description: Registers one or more SDF models for a class of devices.
1042	   If the SDF model is already registered, the gateway will return an
1043	   error with the sdf-model-already-registered problem type.

1045	   Request Body:

1047	   *  The SDF document in JSON format containing one or more sdfThings
1048	      or sdfObjects, similar to the example in Appendix F.

1050	   *  The SDF document MUST contain protocol mappings, as described in
1051	      [I-D.ietf-asdf-sdf-protocol-mapping].

1053	   Response:

1055	   A list containing objects where each object has an "sdfName" which is
1056	   the global name of the top-level sdfThing or sdfObject in the SDF
1057	   model.  The response uses the 201 Created status code.

1059	   <CODE BEGINS> file "sdf_reference.cddl"
1060	   =============== NOTE: '\' line wrapping per RFC 8792 ================

1062	   SdfGlobalName = text    ; absolute URI with fragment referencing an \
1063	                                                   sdfThing or sdfObject

1065	   SdfReference = {
1066	     sdfName: SdfGlobalName
1067	   }

1069	   SdfReferenceArray = [* SdfReference]
1070	   <CODE ENDS>

1072	   Example of a response:

1074	   <CODE BEGINS>
1075	   [
1076	    {
1077	     "sdfName": "https://example.com/heartrate#/sdfObject/healthsensor"
1078	    }
1079	   ]
1080	   <CODE ENDS>

1082	        Figure 6: Example of a response to an SDF model registration

1084	3.1.2.  Get all SDF models

1086	   Method: GET /registrations/models

1088	   Description: Gets all SDF models registered with the gateway.

1090	   Response:

1092	   A list containing objects where each object has an "sdfName" which is
1093	   the global name of the top-level sdfThing or sdfObject in the SDF
1094	   model.

1096	   <CODE BEGINS> file "sdf_reference.cddl"
1097	   =============== NOTE: '\' line wrapping per RFC 8792 ================

1099	   SdfGlobalName = text    ; absolute URI with fragment referencing an \
1100	                                                   sdfThing or sdfObject

1102	   SdfReference = {
1103	     sdfName: SdfGlobalName
1104	   }

1106	   SdfReferenceArray = [* SdfReference]
1107	   <CODE ENDS>

1109	   Example of a response:

1111	   <CODE BEGINS>
1112	   [
1113	    {
1114	     "sdfName": "https://example.com/heartrate#/sdfObject/healthsensor"
1115	    },
1116	    {
1117	     "sdfName": "https://example.com/thermometer#/sdfObject/thermometer"
1118	    }
1119	   ]
1120	   <CODE ENDS>
1121	           Figure 7: Example of a response to get all SDF models

1123	3.1.3.  Get an SDF model

1125	   Method: GET /registrations/models{?sdfName}

1127	   Description: Gets an SDF model registered with the gateway.

1129	   Query Parameters:

1131	   *  sdfName: the name of the top-level sdfThing or sdfObject in the
1132	      SDF model.

1134	   Response:

1136	   *  The SDF model is returned in JSON format, similar to the example
1137	      in Appendix F.

1139	3.1.4.  Delete an SDF model

1150	   Response:

1152	   *  An object with an "sdfName" which is the global name of the top-
1153	      level sdfThing or sdfObject in the SDF model

1155	   <CODE BEGINS> file "sdf_reference.cddl"
1156	   =============== NOTE: '\' line wrapping per RFC 8792 ================

1159	                                                   sdfThing or sdfObject

1161	   SdfReference = {
1162	     sdfName: SdfGlobalName
1163	   }

1165	   SdfReferenceArray = [* SdfReference]
1166	   <CODE ENDS>

1168	   Example of a response:

1170	   <CODE BEGINS>
1171	   {
1172	     "sdfName": "https://example.com/heartrate#/sdfObject/healthsensor"
1173	   }
1174	   <CODE ENDS>

1176	        Figure 8: Example of a response to an SDF model registration

1178	3.1.5.  Update an SDF model

1180	   Method: PUT /registrations/models{?sdfName}

1182	   Description: Updates an SDF model registered with the gateway.

1184	   Query Parameters:

1186	   *  sdfName: the name of the top-level sdfThing or sdfObject in the
1187	      SDF model.

1189	   Request Body:

1191	   *  An SDF model in JSON format, similar to the example in Appendix F.

1193	   Response:

1195	   *  An object with an "sdfName" which is the global name of the top-
1196	      level sdfThing or sdfObject in the SDF model

1198	   <CODE BEGINS> file "sdf_reference.cddl"

1202	                                                   sdfThing or sdfObject

1204	   SdfReference = {
1205	     sdfName: SdfGlobalName
1206	   }

1209	   <CODE ENDS>

1211	   Example of a response:

1213	   <CODE BEGINS>
1214	   {
1215	     "sdfName": "https://example.com/heartrate#/sdfObject/healthsensor"
1216	   }
1217	   <CODE ENDS>
1218	        Figure 9: Example of a response to an SDF model registration

1220	3.2.  Data application registrations APIs

1222	   Data-app registration APIs allow applications to register a data
1223	   application that will receive streaming data from the gateway.  These
1224	   APIs operate on a data app ID.  This ID corresponds to the endpoint
1225	   app ID of the telemetry endpoint app that is registered with the SCIM
1226	   server as an authorized endpoint that can receive data for a device.
1227	   The endpoint app is defined in Section 6 of [RFC9944].

1229	   Apart from the endpoint app ID, the API also configures the protocol
1230	   the data-app supports.  These should be one of the following:

1232	   *  mqttClient: The data-app is an MQTT client, which means that the
1233	      NIPC gateway must act as an MQTT broker.

1235	   *  mqttBroker: The data-app is an MQTT broker, which means that the
1236	      NIPC gateway must act as an MQTT client.  The MQTT broker
1237	      configuration data, such as the URI, credentials and MQTT topic
1238	      must also be provided in the registration API.

1240	   *  webhook: The data-app supports a webhook the gateway can publish
1241	      to.  The registration API has to include the webhook URI &
1242	      credentials.

1244	   *  websocket: The data-app supports a websocket interface.  The
1245	      registration API has to include the websocket URI & credentials.

1247	3.2.1.  Register a data application

1249	   Method: POST /registrations/data-apps{?dataAppId}

1251	   Description: Registers a data application with the gateway.

1253	   Query Parameters:

1255	   *  dataAppId: the ID of the data application

1257	   Request Body:

1259	   *  events: a list of events that the data application is authorized
1260	      for.

1262	   *  mqttClient: a boolean that denotes that the data application is an
1263	      MQTT client.

1265	   *  mqttBroker: The data app is an MQTT broker.  This object contains
1266	      the MQTT broker information:

1268	      -  URI: the URI of the MQTT broker.

1270	      -  username: the username to authenticate with the MQTT broker.

1272	      -  password: the password to authenticate with the MQTT broker.

1274	      -  brokerCACert: the base64-encoded CA certificate of the MQTT
1275	         broker (optional).

1277	      -  customTopic: By default, the topic will be composed of data-
1278	         app/<dataAppId>/<namespace>/<json_pointer_to_sdf_event>, as
1279	         described in Section 4.2.  In this attribute, a custom topic
1280	         can be configured (optional).

1282	   *  webhook: The data app supports a webhook.  This object contains a
1283	      webhook URL along with any credentials that are required to
1284	      authenticate the webhook.

1286	      -  URI: the webhook URI.  The URI MUST include the scheme used by
1287	         the webhook server (e.g., "https://").  It is up to the
1288	         implementation to support different schemes.  If a scheme is
1289	         not supported, the NIPC gateway MUST return an error response
1290	         with type "https://www.iana.org/assignments/nipc-problem-
1291	         types#unsupported-uri-scheme".

1293	      -  headers: An object that contains the headers to be sent with
1294	         the webhook request.  The headers can contain any
1295	         authentication information required by the webhook server.
1296	         Each header is represented as a key-value pair in the object.

1298	      -  serverCACert: the CA certificate of the webhook server, encoded
1299	         as per [RFC7468] and newlines encoded as '\n' (optional)

1301	   *  websocket: The data app supports a websocket.  This object
1302	      contains a websocket URL along with any credentials that are
1303	      required to authenticate the websocket.  The websocket URL is the
1304	      endpoint where the streaming data will be sent.

1306	      -  URI: the websocket URI.  The URI MUST include the scheme used
1307	         by the websocket server (e.g., "wss://").  It is up to the
1308	         implementation to support different schemes.  If a scheme is
1309	         not supported, the NIPC gateway MUST return an error response
1310	         with type "https://www.iana.org/assignments/nipc-problem-
1311	         types#unsupported-uri-scheme".

1313	      -  headers: An object that contains the headers to be sent with
1314	         the websocket request.  The headers can contain any
1315	         authentication information required by the websocket server.
1316	         Each header is represented as a key-value pair in the object.

1318	      -  serverCACert: the CA certificate of the websocket server,
1319	         encoded as per [RFC7468] and newlines encoded as '\n'
1320	         (optional)

1322	   <CODE BEGINS> file "data_app.cddl"
1323	   DataApp = {
1324	     events: [* EventRef],
1325	     ( DataAppMqttClient //
1326	       DataAppMqttBroker //
1327	       DataAppWebhook //
1328	       DataAppWebsocket )
1329	   }

1331	   EventRef = {
1332	     event: text    ; SDF global name (absolute URI with fragment)
1333	   }

1335	   DataAppMqttClient = {
1336	     mqttClient: bool
1337	   }

1339	   DataAppMqttBroker = {
1340	     mqttBroker: {
1341	       URI: text,
1342	       username: text,
1343	       password: text,
1344	       ? brokerCACert: text,   ; PEM-encoded CA certificate
1345	       ? customTopic: text     ; optional custom MQTT topic
1346	     }
1347	   }

1349	   DataAppWebhook = {
1350	     webhook: {
1351	       URI: text,
1352	       ? headers: { * text => text },  ; key/value headers
1353	       ? serverCACert: text
1354	     }
1355	   }

1357	   DataAppWebsocket = {
1358	     websocket: {
1359	       URI: text,
1360	       ? headers: { * text => text },  ; key/value headers
1361	       ? serverCACert: text
1362	     }
1363	   }
1364	   <CODE ENDS>

1366	   Example of a request body:

1368	   <CODE BEGINS>
1369	   =============== NOTE: '\' line wrapping per RFC 8792 ================

1371	   {
1372	     "events": [
1373	       {
1374	         "event": "https://example.com/heartrate#/sdfObject/\
1375	                                     healthsensor/sdfEvent/fallDetected"
1376	       }
1377	     ],
1378	     "mqttClient": true
1379	   }
1380	   <CODE ENDS>

1382	                     Figure 10: Example with mqttClient

1384	   Example of a request body for a data application that is an MQTT
1385	   broker:

1387	   <CODE BEGINS>
1388	   =============== NOTE: '\' line wrapping per RFC 8792 ================

1390	   {
1391	     "events": [
1392	       {
1393	         "event": "https://example.com/heartrate#/sdfObject/\
1394	                                     healthsensor/sdfEvent/fallDetected"
1395	       }
1396	     ],
1397	     "mqttBroker": {
1398	       "URI": "mqtt.example.com:1883",
1399	       "username": "user",
1400	       "password": "password",
1401	       "customTopic": "custom/topic"
1402	     }
1403	   }
1404	   <CODE ENDS>

1406	                     Figure 11: Example with mqttBroker

1408	   Response:

1410	   *  If successful, the response will be identical to the request body.

1412	3.2.2.  Update a data application

1414	   Method: PUT /registrations/data-apps{?dataAppId}
1415	   Description: Updates a data application registration.

1417	   Query Parameters:

1419	   *  dataAppId: the ID of the data application

1421	   Request Body:

1423	   *  The request body is identical to the request body for the register
1424	      data application API Section 3.2.1.

1426	   Response:

1428	   *  If successful, the response will be identical to the request body.

1430	3.2.3.  Get a data application

1432	   Method: GET /registrations/data-apps{?dataAppId}

1434	   Description: Gets a data application object registered with the
1435	   gateway.

1437	   Query Parameters:

1439	   *  dataAppId: the ID of the data application

1441	   Response:

1443	   The response will be identical to the request body for the register
1444	   data application API Section 3.2.1.

1446	3.2.4.  Delete a data application

1448	   Method: DELETE /registrations/data-apps{?dataAppId}

1450	   Description: Deletes a data application registered with the gateway.

1452	   Query Parameters:

1454	   *  dataAppId: the ID of the data application

1456	   Response:

1458	   *  If successful, the response will be 204 No Content.

1460	4.  NIPC Operation APIs

1462	   The NIPC operation APIs perform protocol-neutral interactions on SDF
1463	   affordances, properties, events, and actions.  This allows
1464	   applications to read and update device properties, invoke actions,
1465	   and consume events.

1467	   NIPC defines three API collections aligned with the SDF Affordances
1468	   defined in Section 1.2 of [RFC9880]:

1470	   *  Properties: read and write device properties.

1472	   *  Events: enable and disable device event reporting.

1474	   *  Actions: invoke device actions.

1476	   Additionally, NIPC defines one more API collection that allows
1477	   applications to install triggers on events.  Triggers will trigger an
1478	   action if the event is executed.  Trigger collection:

1480	   *  Triggers: Install a trigger on an event (invokes an action)

1482	   To invoke NIPC operations APIs on a device, one or more SDF models
1483	   MUST be registered for that device.  The SDF model MAY have a
1484	   top-level sdfThing (with multiple sdfObjects) or a top-level
1485	   sdfObject.  Operations depend on affordances (sdfProperty, sdfEvent,
1486	   sdfAction) defined in the registered SDF model and on a device ID
1487	   (see [RFC9944]).  Affordances are referenced by their SDF global name
1488	   (absolute URI with fragment) as described in Section 4 of [RFC9880].

1490	   The NIPC Gateway must match the SDF global name against the
1491	   registered SDF model to resolve the protocol mapping (protocolmap)
1492	   the gateway will execute.  When carried in a URI, the SDF global name
1493	   MUST be percent-encoded per Section 2.1 of [RFC3986].

1495	4.1.  NIPC Property APIs

1497	   These APIs allow applications to read and update device properties.
1498	   If the underlying protocol requires a connection, the gateway
1499	   establishes it implicitly for the operation; when an explicit
1500	   connection is already active, operations reuse it without
1501	   modification.

1503	   Requests and responses support content negotiation via Content-Type
1504	   and Accept.  When using "application/nipc+json", payloads must follow
1505	   the examples above.  Binary property values must be base64-encoded
1506	   with padding per Section 4 of [RFC4648] in the "value" field.  For
1507	   other media types, payload semantics must follow the selected media
1508	   type.

1510	4.1.1.  Update one or multiple values

1512	   Method: PUT /devices/{id}/properties{?propertyName}

1514	   Description: Write values to one or more properties on a device

1516	   Parameters:

1518	   *  id: the ID of the device

1520	   Query Parameters:

1522	   *  propertyName: Identifies a single property to update.  If present,
1523	      the request body MAY use any media type appropriate to the
1524	      property payload.

1526	   -or-

1528	   *  If absent, the request body MUST be application/nipc+json and
1529	      contain an array of update items, each with a property and a
1530	      value.

1532	   Request Body:

1534	   *  If the query parameter propertyName is provided, the request body
1535	      MAY use any media type appropriate to the property payload.  The
1536	      value is encoded as per the content type of the payload.

1538	   -or-

1540	   *  If the query parameter propertyName is NOT provided, the request
1541	      body must be an array of properties to update, each containing a
1542	      property and a value.  The value attribute contains the raw binary
1543	      data, which must be encoded in base64 with padding as per
1544	      Section 4 of [RFC4648].

1546	   <CODE BEGINS> file "property_value_array.cddl"
1547	   PropertyValueArray = [* PropertyValue]

1549	   ; Minimal PropertyValue shape (matches allOf of Property + Value)
1550	   PropertyValue = {
1551	     property: text,        ; SDF global name of the property
1552	     value: b64text         ; base64-encoded bytes (RFC 4648 Section 4)
1553	   }

1555	   ; Helper type for base64-with-padding encoded text
1556	   b64text = text
1557	   <CODE ENDS>

1559	   Example body for updating multiple properties:

1561	   <CODE BEGINS>
1562	   =============== NOTE: '\' line wrapping per RFC 8792 ================

1564	   [
1565	     {
1566	       "property": "https://example.com/heartrate#/sdfObject/thermostat\
1567	                                              /sdfProperty/temperature",
1568	       "value": "dGVzdA=="
1569	     },
1570	     {
1571	       "property": "https://example.com/heartrate#/sdfObject/thermostat\
1572	                                                 /sdfProperty/humidity",
1573	       "value": "eGVzdB=="
1574	     }
1575	   ]
1576	   <CODE ENDS>

1578	              Figure 12: Example updating multiple properties

1580	   Response:

1582	   *  If the Accept header is set to application/nipc+json, the response
1583	      must be an array with a status field set to 200 for each property
1584	      that was updated, or a problem type object for each property that
1585	      failed to update.  The "properties" array must be an array of
1586	      properties that were updated, each containing a property and a
1587	      value.

1589	   -or-

1591	   *  If the Accept header is set to any other media type and the
1592	      propertyName query parameter is provided, the response must be 204
1593	      No Content with no body.

1595	   <CODE BEGINS> file "property_value_response_array.cddl"
1596	   =============== NOTE: '\' line wrapping per RFC 8792 ================

1598	   PropertyValueResponseArray = [* PropertyValueResponseArrayItem]

1600	   PropertyValueResponseArrayItem = ( SuccessResponse // \
1601	                                                       FailureResponse )

1603	   ; Minimal success shape (may be extended)
1604	   SuccessResponse = {
1605	     status: uint
1606	   }
1607	   <CODE ENDS>

1609	   Example of a response:

1611	   <CODE BEGINS>
1612	   =============== NOTE: '\' line wrapping per RFC 8792 ================

1614	   [
1615	     {
1616	       "status": 200
1617	     },
1618	     {
1619	       "type": "https://www.iana.org/assignments/nipc-problem-types#\
1620	                                                 property-not-writable",
1621	       "status": 400,
1622	       "title": "Property Not Writable",
1623	       "detail": "Property https://example.com/heartrate#/sdfObject/\
1624	   thermostat/sdfProperty/temperature does not exist or is not writable"
1625	     }
1626	   ]
1627	   <CODE ENDS>

1629	           Figure 13: Example update multiple properties response

1631	4.1.2.  Read one or multiple values

1633	   Method: GET /devices/{id}/properties{?propertyName*}

1635	   Description: Read values from one or more properties on a device

1637	   Parameters:

1639	   *  id: the ID of the device

1641	   Query Parameters:

1643	   *  propertyName: The property to read.  This can be a single property
1644	      or multiple properties.  If multiple properties are provided, the
1645	      request body MUST contain an application/nipc+json payload with an
1646	      array of properties to read.

1648	   Response:

1650	   *  If the Accept header is set to application/nipc+json, the response
1651	      must be an array of properties, each containing a property and a
1652	      value.  The value must be the raw binary data read from the
1653	      property, encoded in base64 with padding as per Section 4 of
1654	      [RFC4648].  The array must contain objects with 2 attributes: -
1655	      property: The property that was read.  - value: The bytes that
1656	      were read in base64 encoding

1658	   -or-

1660	   *  If the Accept header is set to any other media type and a single
1661	      propertyName query parameter is provided, the request body MAY use
1662	      any media type appropriate to the property payload.  The value is
1663	      encoded as per the content type of the payload.

1665	   <CODE BEGINS> file "property_value_read_response_array.cddl"
1666	   =============== NOTE: '\' line wrapping per RFC 8792 ================

1668	   PropertyValueReadResponseArray = [* \
1669	                                     PropertyValueReadResponseArrayItem]

1671	   PropertyValueReadResponseArrayItem = ( PropertyValue // \
1672	                                                       FailureResponse )
1673	   <CODE ENDS>

1675	   Example of a response:

1677	   <CODE BEGINS>
1678	   =============== NOTE: '\' line wrapping per RFC 8792 ================

1680	   [
1681	     {
1682	       "property": "https://example.com/heartrate#/sdfObject/thermostat\
1683	                                              /sdfProperty/temperature",
1684	       "value": "dGVzdA=="
1685	     },
1686	     {
1687	       "property": "https://example.com/heartrate#/sdfObject/thermostat\
1688	                                                 /sdfProperty/humidity",
1689	       "value": "eGVzdB=="
1690	     }
1691	   ]
1692	   <CODE ENDS>

1694	            Figure 14: Example read multiple properties response

1696	4.2.  NIPC Event APIs

1698	   Event APIs enable or disable reporting of device events.  For certain
1699	   protocols, a connection may be required.  If the underlying protocol
1700	   requires a connection, the gateway establishes it implicitly for the
1701	   operation.  If an explicitly created connection is already active, it
1702	   is reused without modification.

1704	   Events are referenced by the SDF global name of an sdfEvent.  The
1705	   {id} path segment identifies a device or a group of devices.  A group
1706	   event MAY be enabled only if the underlying protocol supports group
1707	   activation (e.g., BLE advertisement or connection status events).

1709	   Events are delivered to registered data-apps over a publish/subscribe
1710	   interface, as defined in Section 7.  If the data application
1711	   registered for this event is an MQTT broker or client, the event SDF
1712	   global name may be used to construct the MQTT topic for the event.
1713	   The topic is constructed using the data application ID, the default
1714	   namespace for the event, and the event itself.  For example, if the
1715	   data application ID is "0927ce7c-b258-4bfa-a345-bcc9f74385b4" and the
1716	   event is
1717	   "https://example.com/thermometer#/sdfThing/thermometer/sdfEvent/
1718	   isPresent", the topic will be:

1720	   =============== NOTE: '\' line wrapping per RFC 8792 ================

1722	   data-app/<dataAppId>/<namespace>/<json_pointer_to_sdf_event>

1724	   data-app/0927ce7c-b258-4bfa-a345-bcc9f74385b4/thermometer/sdfThing/\
1725	                                          thermometer/sdfEvent/isPresent

1727	   A data application may subscribe to this topic using the topic or it
1728	   may use MQTT wildcards to subscribe to data-app/+/temperature/# to
1729	   receive all events for the temperature namespace.

1731	   If a customTopic was supplied in the data-app registration
1732	   (mqttBroker case), that topic MUST be used instead of the constructed
1733	   default.

1735	4.2.1.  Enable event reporting

1737	   Method: POST /devices/{id}/events{?eventName}

1739	   Description: Enables an event on a device

1741	   Parameters:

1743	   *  id: the ID of the device

1745	   Query Parameters:

1747	   *  eventName: the event to enable.  The eventName must be a URL
1748	      encoded string that is the absolute URI that is the global name of
1749	      an sdfEvent.

1751	   Response:

1753	   *  Returns HTTP status code 201 Created with a Location header
1754	      pointing to the created event instance.

1756	   Example of a successful response:

1758	   =============== NOTE: '\' line wrapping per RFC 8792 ================

1760	   HTTP/1.1 201 Created
1761	   Location: /devices/1d3b2c36-8a65-45a6-87c1-bcdbe0a32e30/events?\
1762	                         instanceId=02ee282c-8915-4b2e-bbd2-88966773134a

1764	   The Location header must contain the URI for the created event
1765	   instance, which may be used to check status or disable the event.

1767	4.2.2.  Disable event reporting

1769	   Method: DELETE /devices/{id}/events{?instanceId}

1771	   Description: Disables an event on a device

1773	   Parameters:

1775	   *  id: the ID of the device or group of devices

1777	   Query Parameters:

1779	   *  instanceId: the instance ID of the event to disable (obtained from
1780	      the Location header when the event was enabled)

1782	   Response:

1784	   *  Returns HTTP status code 204 No Content on successful disable.

1786	   HTTP/1.1 204 No Content

1788	4.2.3.  Get status of one or more events

1790	   Method: GET /devices/{id}/events{?instanceId*}

1792	   Description: Get the status of one or more events on a specific
1793	   device

1795	   Parameters:

1797	   *  id: the ID of the device or group of devices

1799	   Query Parameters:

1801	   *  instanceId: a comma separated list of event instance IDs to filter
1802	      by (optional)

1804	   Response: The response must be an array of events, each containing an
1805	   instanceID and an event.  - instanceId: must be the unique instance
1806	   ID for each enabled event.  - event: must be the event URI for each
1807	   enabled event.

1809	   <CODE BEGINS> file "event_status_array.cddl"
1810	   =============== NOTE: '\' line wrapping per RFC 8792 ================

1812	   EventStatusResponseArray = [* EventStatusResponseArrayItem]

1814	   EventStatusResponseArrayItem = ( EventInstanceSuccess // \
1815	                                                       FailureResponse )

1817	   ; Success item = { event, instanceId }
1818	   EventInstanceSuccess = {
1819	     event: text,        ; SDF global name of the event (absolute URI \
1820	                                                          with fragment)
1821	     instanceId: text    ; UUID (as text)
1822	   }
1823	   <CODE ENDS>

1825	   Example of a response:

1827	   <CODE BEGINS>
1828	   =============== NOTE: '\' line wrapping per RFC 8792 ================

1830	   [
1831	     {
1832	       "instanceId": "02ee282c-8915-4b2e-bbd2-88966773134a",
1833	       "event": "https://example.com/heartrate#/sdfObject/healthsensor/\
1834	                                                  sdfEvent/fallDetected"
1835	     }
1836	   ]
1837	   <CODE ENDS>

1839	           Figure 15: Example get multiple events status response

1841	4.2.4.  Enable event reporting on a group of devices

1843	   Method: POST /groups/{id}/events

1845	   Description: Enables an event on a group of devices

1847	   Parameters:

1849	   *  id: the ID of the group of devices

1851	   Query Parameters:

1853	   *  eventName: the event to enable.  The eventName is a URL encoded
1854	      string that is the absolute URI that is the global name of an
1855	      sdfEvent.

1857	   Response:

1859	   *  The response must return HTTP status code 201 Created with a
1860	      Location header pointing to the created event instance.  The
1861	      Location header must contain the URI for the created event
1862	      instance, which can be used to check status or disable the event.

1864	   Example of a successful response:

1866	   =============== NOTE: '\' line wrapping per RFC 8792 ================

1868	   HTTP/1.1 201 Created
1869	   Location: /groups/0dc729d7-f6c3-491d-9b9d-e7176d2be243/events?\
1870	                         instanceId=f1b9f26b-21ce-4deb-9d57-289ba7e17cce

1872	4.2.5.  Disable event reporting on a group of devices

1874	   Method: DELETE /groups/{id}/events{?instanceId}

1876	   Description: Disables an event on a group of devices

1878	   Parameters:

1880	   *  id: the ID of the group of devices

1882	   Query Parameters:

1884	   *  instanceId: the instance ID of the event to disable (obtained from
1885	      the Location header when the event was enabled)

1887	   Response:

1889	   MUST return 200 OK with an array of per-device event status entries.
1890	   For each device where the event was successfully disabled, the entry
1891	   MUST include deviceId and event (SDF global name).  For each device
1892	   where disabling failed, the entry MUST be a Problem Details error
1893	   object for that device.

1895	   <CODE BEGINS> file "group_event_status_response_array.cddl"

1900	   GroupEventSuccessResponse = { event: text, deviceId: text }

1902	   ; Each item is either an event+deviceId success or a \
1903	                                                    GroupFailureResponse
1904	   GroupEventStatusResponse = (GroupEventSuccessResponse // \
1905	                                                   GroupFailureResponse)

1907	   GroupFailureResponse = {
1908	     FailureResponse,
1909	     ? deviceId: text
1910	   }
1911	   <CODE ENDS>

1913	   Example of a response:

1915	   =============== NOTE: '\' line wrapping per RFC 8792 ================

1917	   [
1918	     {
1919	       "event": "https://example.com/heartrate#/sdfObject/healthsensor/\
1920	                                                 sdfEvent/fallDetected",

1922	     },
1923	     {
1924	       "event": "https://example.com/heartrate#/sdfObject/healthsensor/\
1925	                                                 sdfEvent/fallDetected",
1926	       "deviceId": "d62c7fb2-a216-4811-a388-053b17fdbedc"
1927	     },
1928	     {
1929	       "event": "https://example.com/heartrate#/sdfObject/healthsensor/\
1930	                                                 sdfEvent/fallDetected",

1932	     },

1935	                                                     event-not-enabled",
1936	       "status": 400,
1937	       "title": "Event Not Enabled",
1938	       "deviceId": "9171ec16-e3c1-4ccf-ad23-b92a1a3f069d",
1939	       "detail": "Failed to disable the event for device 9171ec16-e3c1-\

1941	     }
